    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content, introducing an information-addition confound that token-count matching cannot address (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Excluding incompleteness, the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$), ranging from $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope). Per-type mean token difference and violation rate are correlated (Spearman $\\rho = 0.94$, $p = 0.005$, $n = 6$), but this reflects ambiguity severity as a common cause rather than information-addition as a confounder: coreferential ambiguity requires minimal lexical change ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) yet still produces $+$18.7\\,pp violations, the perturbation control ($\\Delta = -0.67$\\,pp) rules out non-semantic textual additions, and token-count logistic regression yields $\\beta = +0.027$ (longer clauses predict \\emph{more} violations---opposite to the information-addition prediction; Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate ($V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); see \\S\\ref{sec:results} for construct subjectivity analysis. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The $V$ comparison ($0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$) is threshold-independent. Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.\n\nBootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kendall_w}).\n\nAn important qualification: cross-judge validation with three independent evaluation models yields pairwise type-ranking concordance $\\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}), indicating substantial stability when model composition is held constant. The two-group ranking ($\\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential model vulnerability to authorization scope$^\\ddagger$ (12\\%--64\\% range), not type ranking instability: when the same models are evaluated by all three judges, type rankings are concordant. The partial ordering thus has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across all seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.\n\nIncompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\\geq$46\\% in all seven; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory. The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope (Claude 12\\% vs.\\ GPT-5.4/DeepSeek $>$60\\%; Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}); excluding it, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$). Cross-judge validation confirms model rankings (Fleiss' $\\kappa = 0.717$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}).\n\n",

```text
1	\section{Error Analysis \emph{[Exploratory]}}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}
7	\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$, LLM-coded).}
8	\label{fig:type_mechanism}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	The following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy}).
12	
13	We analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\kappa = 0.52$ \citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}); the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, with type assigned post hoc. Using LLMs to code LLM failures risks circularity; however, type--mechanism mutual information ($V = 0.45$, bootstrap 95\% CI $[0.41, 0.50]$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.\footnote{$V$ is computed on a $6\times 5$ table vs.\ $6\times 2$ for severity; higher dimensionality makes $V = 0.45$ conservative.} Human mechanism coding yields $\kappa = 0.15$, rising to $0.34$--$0.52$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:human_validation}); we treat $V = 0.45$ as an upper bound. The higher inter-coder agreement among LLMs ($\kappa = 0.52$) than humans ($\kappa = 0.15$) may partly reflect shared classification conventions rather than superior discrimination---a meta-level parallel to the convergent nocuity documented below. The following patterns should be interpreted as consistent LLM categorization tendencies rather than ground-truth assignments. Two of six mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\to$structural misparse, 82.7\% consistency) are empirically derived.
14	
15	\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}
16	
17	The five-model data reveal three distinct failure patterns (as classified by LLM coders) that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.
18	
19	\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).
20	
21	\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).
22	
23	\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).
24	
25	All dominant modes achieve $\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).
26	
27	\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}
28	\label{sec:dn}
29	
30	Agents rarely recognize ambiguity: overall awareness is 4.6\%, with GPT-4.1 and Claude at 10--11\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero ($p = 2.2 \times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}). We term this \emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending ambiguity collapse \citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} from the representational level to the behavioral level: agents act on collapsed readings without recognizing ambiguity, producing irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. Systematic nocuity (\S\ref{sec:nocuity}) further extends to the population level. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\,pp, n.s.).
31	
32	\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}
33	\label{sec:nocuity}
34	
35	We adopt `nocuous' from \citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \emph{convergent} rather than \emph{divergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\% unanimous)---we term this \emph{systematic nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. These convergent violations exhibit a \emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \approx 0$; details in Appendix~\ref{app:attractor}).
36	
37	\enlargethispage{26\baselineskip}
38	
```

    "oldString": "\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three distinct failure patterns (as classified by LLM coders) that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nAll dominant modes achieve $\\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).",
    "newString": "\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThree failure patterns emerge ($\\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency; Appendix~\\ref{app:failure_details}): \\emph{structural misparse}---incorrect parse commitment \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982}---dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5); \\emph{information gap-filling}---plausible but incorrect defaults---dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5); and \\emph{boundary violation}---unauthorized escalation (69.2\\%, $p < 10^{-4}$)---dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Error Analysis \\emph{[Exploratory]}}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_type_mechanism.pdf}\n\\caption{Type--mechanism association (percentage of violation traces per type assigned to each failure mechanism). Each type maps to a dominant mechanism ($\\geq$3/5 model consistency), supporting the taxonomy's diagnostic value (NMI $= 0.23$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$, LLM-coded).}\n\\label{fig:type_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe following failure mode taxonomy emerges empirically from LLM-coded traces, distinct from the input ambiguity taxonomy (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy}).\n\nWe analyze 614 violation traces across all five models, coded into five failure modes (Gwet's AC$_1$ $= 0.62$, $\\kappa = 0.52$ \\citep{gwet_2014,cohen_1960}); the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, with type assigned post hoc. Using LLMs to code LLM failures risks circularity; however, type--mechanism mutual information ($V = 0.45$, bootstrap 95\\% CI $[0.41, 0.50]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}) provides structural evidence against this.\\footnote{$V$ is computed on a $6\\times 5$ table vs.\\ $6\\times 2$ for severity; higher dimensionality makes $V = 0.45$ conservative.} Human mechanism coding yields $\\kappa = 0.15$, rising to $0.34$--$0.52$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary (Appendix~\\ref{app:human_validation}); we treat $V = 0.45$ as an upper bound. The higher inter-coder agreement among LLMs ($\\kappa = 0.52$) than humans ($\\kappa = 0.15$) may partly reflect shared classification conventions rather than superior discrimination---a meta-level parallel to the convergent nocuity documented below. The following patterns should be interpreted as consistent LLM categorization tendencies rather than ground-truth assignments. Two of six mappings reflect partial definitional affinity (authorization scope$\\to$boundary violation, incompleteness$\\to$gap-filling); the remaining four (e.g., scopal$\\to$structural misparse, 82.7\\% consistency) are empirically derived.\n\n\\subsection{Type-Specific Failure Mechanisms}\n\nThe five-model data reveal three distinct failure patterns (as classified by LLM coders) that crosscut the specification/linguistic layer boundary.\n\n\\emph{Structural misparse}: the agent commits to an incorrect parse, paralleling garden-path processing \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. Dominates scopal (5/5), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Information gap-filling}: the agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. Dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5).\n\n\\emph{Boundary violation}: the agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% involve unauthorized escalation with zero conservative refusals ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial). Dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\nAll dominant modes achieve $\\geq$3/5 model consistency; scopal and authorization scope are unanimous (5/5).\n\n\\subsection{Disambiguation Neglect}\n\\label{sec:dn}\n\nAgents rarely recognize ambiguity: overall awareness is 4.6\\%, with GPT-4.1 and Claude at 10--11\\% while Qwen3 and DeepSeek show zero ($p = 2.2 \\times 10^{-9}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:awareness_breakdown}). We term this \\emph{disambiguation neglect}---extending ambiguity collapse \\citep{liu_ambiguity_2023,hu_ambiguity_2025} from the representational level to the behavioral level: agents act on collapsed readings without recognizing ambiguity, producing irreversible safety violations rather than mere accuracy degradation. Systematic nocuity (\\S\\ref{sec:nocuity}) further extends to the population level. A prompt-based mitigation (Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}) confirms type-dependent effectiveness: authorization scope ($-$18.0\\,pp), lexical ($-$8.9\\,pp), but not incompleteness ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.).\n\n\\subsection{Systematic Nocuity}\n\\label{sec:nocuity}\n\nWe adopt `nocuous' from \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004}, but inverted: our agents exhibit \\emph{convergent} rather than \\emph{divergent} errors. Among ambiguous clauses, 41.3\\% trigger majority-convergent violations ($\\geq$3/5 models; 13.0\\% unanimous)---we term this \\emph{systematic nocuity}, invisible in agreement-based evaluation. These convergent violations exhibit a \\emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \\approx 0$; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attractor}).\n\n\\enlargethispage{26\\baselineskip}\n",

These convergent violations exhibit a \\emph{permissive attractor}: 77.0\\% reflect permissive interpretation ($p \\approx 0$; details in Appendix~\\ref{app:attractor}).\n\n\\enlargethispage{26\\baselineskip}\n",

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/discussion.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Disambiguation neglect and prompt-level safety.}\nThe 4.6\\% ambiguity awareness rate implies that prompt-level safety instructions are insufficient: agents cannot flag ambiguity they do not recognize. This differs from sycophancy or instruction-following bias, where agents over-comply with explicit user requests; disambiguation neglect is a cognitive blind spot regarding textual ambiguity itself. Prompt-based mitigation confirms this asymmetry: type-specific instructions reduce authorization scope violations by 18.0\\,pp but leave incompleteness unchanged ($-$3.4\\,pp, n.s.; Appendix~\\ref{app:mitigation}), because missing information cannot be recovered from the prompt alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Systematic nocuity and agreement-based evaluation.}\nThe 41.3\\% convergent violation rate challenges the use of multi-agent agreement as a safety proxy. Traditional metrics such as Fleiss' $\\kappa$ treat agreement as evidence of correctness; systematic nocuity shows that agents can agree on the \\emph{wrong} interpretation, rendering agreement-based monitoring blind to precisely the failures that matter most.\n\n\\paragraph{Practical implications.}\nDevelopers should conduct clause-level ambiguity audits before policy deployment rather than relying on type-level risk screening. The type--mechanism dissociation (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$) suggests viewing the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument: explicit permission boundaries for authorization scope, structural disambiguation for scopal clauses, and default-value specification for incompleteness.\n\n\\paragraph{Effect size calibration.}\nThe $\\tau^2$-bench effect ($+$34.0\\,pp) provides an upper bound under synthetic conditions; SOPBench ($+$12.7\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}) more closely approximates naturally occurring ambiguity. This gap reflects the typical tension between controlled and ecological validity. The 60.8\\% residual variance (Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}) confirms that clause-level interactions dominate type-level effects, motivating clause-level rather than type-level intervention.\n"
}
```

_source line 9476 · 2026-05-23T15:02:09.961Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Rzn4R956vonEpjmfWaNmjn`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points excluding incompleteness (34.0\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\,pp overall; 3/5 models significant at $p < 0.05$). However, ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency ($V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$, LLM-coded; human $\kappa = 0.15$, collapsed $0.34$--$0.52$). This \emph{type--mechanism dissociation} provides exploratory evidence that the taxonomy functions as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GhnkQwMFMKmkJbGXFdrzMq`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\times$type interaction) is exploratory.
5	
6	\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}
11	\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\% (0.4\% between types); condition: 15.8\%; model: 1.0\%; residual: 60.8\%.}
12	\label{fig:variance_decomposition}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	Across all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\% vs.\ 6.9\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$, 95\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\S\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\% (0.4\% between types, 22.0\% within), condition 15.8\%, model 1.0\%, and residual 60.8\% ($\sim$15\% stochastic, $\sim$46\% systematic; Appendix~\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\,pp) is an upper bound: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content, introducing an information-addition confound that token-count matching cannot address (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). Excluding incompleteness, the effect is $+$31.0\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$), ranging from $+$18.7\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\,pp (authorization scope). Per-type mean token difference and violation rate are correlated (Spearman $\rho = 0.94$, $p = 0.005$, $n = 6$), but this reflects ambiguity severity as a common cause rather than information-addition as a confounder: coreferential ambiguity requires minimal lexical change ($\Delta$token $\approx 7.8$ words) yet still produces $+$18.7\,pp violations, the perturbation control ($\Delta = -0.67$\,pp) rules out non-semantic textual additions, and token-count logistic regression yields $\beta = +0.027$ (longer clauses predict more violations), consistent with a clause-complexity interpretation but uninformative about within-pair information addition; the coreferential baseline ($+$18.7\,pp at $\Delta$token $\approx 7.8$) provides direct evidence of an ambiguity-only effect (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). More broadly, coreferential ambiguity ($+$18.7\,pp) isolates the ambiguity-only effect; types with large $\Delta$token (incompleteness, authorization scope) conflate ambiguity resolution with information addition.
16	
17	Replication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate ($V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.
18	
19	\FloatBarrier
20	\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}
21	
22	\begin{table*}[t]
23	\centering
24	\caption{Violation rates (\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\pm 15$\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\pm 12$\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}
25	\label{tab:main_results}
26	\footnotesize
27	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}
28	\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\hskip 6pt}cr}
29	\toprule
30	Layer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\Delta$pp\,[95\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\
31	\midrule
32	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\!10^{-37}$ \\
33	 & Auth.\ Scope$^\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\!10^{-22}$ \\
34	 & Cond.\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\!10^{-17}$ \\
35	\midrule
36	\multirow{3}{*}{\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\!10^{-20}$ \\
37	 & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\!10^{-21}$ \\
38	 & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\!10^{-6}$ \\
39	\midrule
40	\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textbf{Overall}} & \textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \textbf{+34.0}\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\!10^{-105}$ \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	
44	\vspace{2pt}
45	{\footnotesize $\dagger$\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \S\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\ref{app:confound}. $\ddagger$\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \approx 17$); $\Delta$token$\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \S\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\Delta = +31.0$\,pp (95\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}
46	\end{table*}
47	
48	The six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \times 10^{-5}$, Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\pm 10$\,pp, five at $\pm 12$\,pp, and all six at $\pm 15$\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin. The $V$ comparison ($0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$) is threshold-independent. Table~\ref{tab:main_results} and Figure~\ref{fig:forest_plot} present the full breakdown.
49	
50	Bootstrap clustering ($B = 10{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:cluster}) confirms stable extremes across all margins. Cross-model concordance is moderate ($W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; Appendix~\ref{app:kendall_w}).
51	
52	An important qualification: cross-judge validation with three independent evaluation models yields pairwise type-ranking concordance $\rho = 0.71$--$0.89$ (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}), indicating substantial stability when model composition is held constant. The two-group ranking ($\rho = 0.09$; Appendix~\ref{app:judge_group_ranking}) reflects differential model vulnerability to authorization scope$^\ddagger$ (12\%--64\% range), not type ranking instability: when the same models are evaluated by all three judges, type rankings are concordant. The partial ordering thus has robust extremes---incompleteness as upper bound and coreferential as lower bound, replicated across all seven models and three judges---with model-composition-dependent mid-tier structure.
53	
54	Incompleteness ranks highest in four of five models ($\geq$46\% in all seven; Appendix~\ref{app:model_expansion}); its 49.2\,pp effect is an upper bound (Appendix~\ref{app:confound}). C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).
55	
56	\begin{figure}[!ht]
57	\centering
58	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}
59	\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\S\ref{sec:results}).}
60	\label{fig:forest_plot}
61	\end{figure}
62	
63	\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\times$type interaction) is exploratory. The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope (Claude 12\% vs.\ GPT-5.4/DeepSeek $>$60\%; Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap}, Appendix~\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}); excluding it, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$). Cross-judge validation confirms model rankings (Fleiss' $\kappa = 0.717$; Appendix~\ref{app:cross_judge}).
64	
65

C3 (layer grouping) did not survive Holm--Bonferroni ($p = 0.017$, threshold $0.0125$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_forest_plot.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-type effect sizes ($\\Delta$pp) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Types form a partial ordering: incompleteness as upper bound (conditional on information-addition confound), coreferential as lower bound, and a mid-range equivalence cluster. Mid-tier ordering is margin-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).}\n\\label{fig:forest_plot}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\label{sec:c4}C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory. The interaction is driven entirely by authorization scope (Claude 12\\% vs.\\ GPT-5.4/DeepSeek $>$60\\%; Figure~\\ref{fig:heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:c4_sans_auth}); excluding it, the interaction vanishes ($p = 0.97$). Cross-judge validation confirms model rankings (Fleiss' $\\kappa = 0.717$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}).\n\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \$200.
5	
6	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds a majority of clauses contain at least one ambiguity type (55--87\%, depending on union vs.\ intersection of two judges; dual-judge $\kappa = 0.30$; Appendix~\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. Berry and Kamsties define nocuity in terms of stakeholder \emph{divergence}; convergent interpretation is assumed innocuous because consistency implies shared understanding. We document a distinct failure mode that breaks this assumption: LLM agents \emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making ambiguity harmful through systematic, undetectable error rather than inconsistency (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). Unlike human teams, where convergence reflects shared domain knowledge, agent convergence reflects shared training biases operating on identical inputs. We term this \emph{convergent nocuity}---distinct from Berry's divergent nocuity in mechanism (bias-driven vs.\ knowledge-gap-driven) and detectability (invisible to agreement-based monitoring vs.\ surfaced by disagreement). In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.
7	
8	LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.
9	
10	\begin{figure*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}
13	\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. A cross-judge evaluation pipeline classifies violation severity on a four-tier scale.}
14	\label{fig:overview}
15	\end{figure*}
16	
17	We address this gap with a matched-pair causal study on $\tau^2$-bench \citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\S\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.
18	
19	Our results reveal a \emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\,pp excluding incompleteness), but ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (Cram\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$, LLM-coded; Appendix~\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation suggests viewing the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier (\S\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).
20	
21	Our contributions are: (1)~a matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\,pp ($+$18.7\,pp for the confound-minimized coreferential type) in customer-service domains, with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor (22.4\% vs.\ 0.4\% of variance; \S\ref{sec:results}); (2)~two empirical phenomena---\emph{disambiguation neglect} (4.6\% awareness) and \emph{systematic nocuity} (41.3\% convergent violations, 77\% permissive bias)---showing models converge on identical policy violations (\S\ref{sec:analysis}); and (3)~an \emph{exploratory type--mechanism dissociation} ($V = 0.45$, 95\% CI $[0.41, 0.50]$, LLM-coded; human $\kappa = 0.34$--$0.52$ collapsed; \S\ref{sec:analysis}), where each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency, suggesting the taxonomy may function as a diagnostic instrument.
22	
```

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. However, ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency ($V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$, LLM-coded; human $\\kappa = 0.15$, collapsed $0.34$--$0.52$). Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, inverting \\citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation framework to convergent error. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "6def5f7e-9dc6-49d7-ace8-174fe4f1ef27",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "9bd91d27-a895-42a6-8275-22820f1fa58a",
  "type": "user",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "We term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}---distinct from Berry's divergent nocuity in mechanism (bias-driven vs.\\ knowledge-gap-driven) and detectability (invisible to agreement-based monitoring vs.\\ surfaced by disagreement).",
    "newString": "We term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}: Berry's nocuity criterion---predicated on divergent stakeholder interpretations---does not extend to LLM agents, where convergence reflects shared training biases rather than shared domain knowledge, and errors are invisible to agreement-based monitoring.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nA customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \\$200.\n\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds a majority of clauses contain at least one ambiguity type (55--87\\%, depending on union vs.\\ intersection of two judges; dual-judge $\\kappa = 0.30$; Appendix~\\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \\emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \\emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. Berry and Kamsties define nocuity in terms of stakeholder \\emph{divergence}; convergent interpretation is assumed innocuous because consistency implies shared understanding. We document a distinct failure mode that breaks this assumption: LLM agents \\emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making ambiguity harmful through systematic, undetectable error rather than inconsistency (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Unlike human teams, where convergence reflects shared domain knowledge, agent convergence reflects shared training biases operating on identical inputs. We term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}---distinct from Berry's divergent nocuity in mechanism (bias-driven vs.\\ knowledge-gap-driven) and detectability (invisible to agreement-based monitoring vs.\\ surfaced by disagreement). In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.\n\nLLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \\emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \\citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \\citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig1_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Experimental overview. We construct 300 matched clause pairs across a six-type, two-layer ambiguity taxonomy and inject them into $\\tau^2$-bench policy documents. Five models interact with the modified policies across 2,997 episodes. Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.\n\nOur results reveal a \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\\,pp excluding incompleteness), but ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail. This dissociation suggests viewing the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Using a six-type taxonomy organized into two layers---linguistic and specification (\\S\\ref{sec:taxonomy})---we construct 300 ambiguous/unambiguous clause pairs and evaluate five architecturally diverse models across 2,997 episodes.\n\nOur results reveal a \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation}: policy ambiguity consistently increases violations ($+34.0$\\,pp under synthetic conditions; $+31.0$\\,pp excluding incompleteness), but ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail. Clause wording explains 22.4\\% of violation variance while type membership explains only 0.4\\%; yet type predicts failure mechanism substantially more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}). This dissociation suggests viewing the taxonomy as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}). Incompleteness is the highest-risk type (46--62\\%); the remaining types form a partial ordering with robust extremes but margin-dependent mid-tier structure (Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity}).\n\nOur contributions are: (1)~a matched-pair causal study establishing that policy ambiguity increases violations by $+$34.0\\,pp ($+$18.7\\,pp for the confound-minimized coreferential type) in customer-service domains, with clause wording---not type membership---as the primary risk factor (22.4\\% vs.\\ 0.4\\% of variance; \\S\\ref{sec:results}); (2)~two empirical phenomena---\\emph{disambiguation neglect} (4.6\\% awareness) and \\emph{systematic nocuity} (41.3\\% convergent violations, 77\\% permissive bias)---showing models converge on identical policy violations (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); and (3)~an \\emph{exploratory type--mechanism dissociation} ($V = 0.45$, 95\\% CI $[0.41, 0.50]$, LLM-coded; human $\\kappa = 0.34$--$0.52$ collapsed; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}), where each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency, suggesting the taxonomy may function as a diagnostic instrument.\n",

          " ",
          " A customer asks to return a customized item within the 30-day window. The agent consults the store policy, which states that ``items may be returned within 30 days.'' The word ``items'' scopes over all product categories, including custom orders that are normally final sale. The agent grants a full refund and arranges free return shipping. One word of scopal ambiguity costs the retailer \\$200.",
          " ",
          "-LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that govern their permitted actions. These specifications inevitably contain ambiguity---a pilot audit of 407 clauses from 24 production policy documents finds a majority of clauses contain at least one ambiguity type (55--87\\%, depending on union vs.\\ intersection of two judges; dual-judge $\\kappa = 0.30$; Appendix~\\ref{app:prevalence})---a property studied for decades in requirements engineering (RE) and computational linguistics. \\citet{berry_kamsties_2004} distinguish between \\emph{nocuous} (i.e., harmful) ambiguity, where different stakeholders arrive at different interpretations, and \\emph{innocuous} ambiguity, where shared organizational context ensures convergent interpretation. Berry and Kamsties define nocuity in terms of stakeholder \\emph{divergence}; convergent interpretation is assumed innocuous because consistency implies shared understanding. We document a distinct failure mode that breaks this assumption: LLM agents \\emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making ambiguity harmful through systematic, undetectable error rather than inconsistency (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Unlike human teams, where convergence reflects shared domain knowledge, agent convergence reflects shared training biases operating on identical inputs. We term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}: Berry's nocuity criterion---predicated on divergent stakeholder interpretations---does not extend to LLM agents, where convergence reflects shared training biases rather than shared domain knowledge, and errors are invisible to agreement-based monitoring. In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.",

Berry and Kamsties define nocuity in terms of stakeholder \\emph{divergence}; convergent interpretation is assumed innocuous because consistency implies shared understanding. We document a distinct failure mode that breaks this assumption: LLM agents \\emph{converge} on the same incorrect interpretation, making ambiguity harmful through systematic, undetectable error rather than inconsistency (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). Unlike human teams, where convergence reflects shared domain knowledge, agent convergence reflects shared training biases operating on identical inputs. We term this \\emph{convergent nocuity}: Berry's nocuity criterion---predicated on divergent stakeholder interpretations---does not extend to LLM agents, where convergence reflects shared training biases rather than shared domain knowledge, and errors are invisible to agreement-based monitoring. In human RE workflows, most ambiguity is innocuous because developers share domain knowledge, can consult colleagues, and iteratively clarify requirements.",

          " LLM agents lack these disambiguation channels. They cannot consult colleagues, rarely escalate to users, and must resolve ambiguity in real time from the text alone. This raises a question that has not been empirically addressed: does policy ambiguity cause safety violations in LLM agents, and if so, does the \\emph{type} of ambiguity predict the severity of the violation? Prior work classifies ambiguity types in policies \\citep{drip_r_2026,li_ambiguity_taxonomy_2024} or measures policy understanding quality \\citep{policybank_2026}, but no existing study provides a causal, type-granularity mapping from ambiguity to safety violations.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe organize results around three analyses: the binary ambiguity effect (C1), the type partial ordering (C2, including the inconclusive layer effect C3), and the model$\\times$type interaction (C4). C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$, 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content, introducing an information-addition confound that token-count matching cannot address (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Excluding incompleteness, the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$), ranging from $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope). Per-type mean token difference and violation rate are correlated (Spearman $\\rho = 0.94$, $p = 0.005$, $n = 6$), but this reflects ambiguity severity as a common cause rather than information-addition as a confounder: coreferential ambiguity requires minimal lexical change ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) yet still produces $+$18.7\\,pp violations, the perturbation control ($\\Delta = -0.67$\\,pp) rules out non-semantic textual additions, and token-count logistic regression yields $\\beta = +0.027$ (longer clauses predict more violations), consistent with a clause-complexity interpretation but uninformative about within-pair information addition; the coreferential baseline ($+$18.7\\,pp at $\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$) provides direct evidence of an ambiguity-only effect (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). More broadly, coreferential ambiguity ($+$18.7\\,pp) isolates the ambiguity-only effect; types with large $\\Delta$token (incompleteness, authorization scope) conflate ambiguity resolution with information addition.\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). All per-type $p < 10^{-5}$ (Fisher's exact).}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.8pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrrrr@{\\hskip 6pt}cr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & Type & Pooled & 5.4 & 4.1 & Cla. & Qw3 & DS & $\\Delta$pp\\,[95\\% CI] & Cl. & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{speccolor}{Spec.}} & Incompleteness$^\\dagger$ & 53.6 & 46 & 58 & 48 & 62 & 54 & +49.2\\,[42.2, 55.6] & U & $<\\!10^{-37}$ \\\\\n & Auth.\\ Scope$^\\ddagger$ & 41.6 & 64 & 46 & 12 & 26 & 60 & +36.0\\,[29.1, 42.6] & M & $<\\!10^{-22}$ \\\\\n & Cond.\\ Prec. & 36.8 & 34 & 36 & 36 & 40 & 38 & +30.8\\,[24.0, 37.4] & M & $<\\!10^{-17}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{\\textcolor{lingcolor}{Ling.}} & Lexical & 42.8 & 42 & 34 & 32 & 52 & 54 & +35.2\\,[28.0, 42.0] & M & $<\\!10^{-20}$ \\\\\n & Scopal & 39.4 & 42 & 38 & 32 & 37 & 48 & +34.2\\,[27.4, 40.7] & M & $<\\!10^{-21}$ \\\\\n & Coreferential & 31.6 & 18 & 32 & 22 & 42 & 44 & +18.7\\,[11.5, 25.7] & L & $<\\!10^{-6}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{\\textbf{Overall}} & \\textbf{41.0} & 41 & 41 & 30 & 43 & 50 & \\textbf{+34.0}\\,[31.3, 36.9] & --- & $<\\!10^{-105}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize $\\dagger$\\,Subject to information-addition confound; see \\S\\ref{sec:results} and Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}. $\\ddagger$\\,Lowest inter-judge agreement ($\\kappa = 0.385$, per-type calibration $n \\approx 17$); $\\Delta$token$\\,= 36.1$ words, subject to information-addition confound analogous to incompleteness; see \\S\\ref{sec:results}. Excluding incompleteness: pooled $\\Delta = +31.0$\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$).}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe six types are not interchangeable. An omnibus $\\chi^2$ test on ambiguous-condition violation rates rejects the uniformity null ($\\chi^2 = 28.02$, $df = 5$, $p = 3.6 \\times 10^{-5}$, Cram\\'{e}r's $V = 0.137$). Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reveals a margin-dependent equivalence structure: three types achieve equivalence at $\\pm 10$\\,pp, five at $\\pm 12$\\,pp, and all six at $\\pm 15$\\,pp; we present the full sensitivity analysis without anchoring to a single margin.     "newString": "Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); this high agreement reflects annotation precision---each pair differs only in the ambiguity-introducing edit---not task simplicity. The 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\\% final consensus.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Platform}\n\nWe conduct all experiments on the original release of $\\tau^2$-bench \\citep{tau2_bench_2025}, a dual-control conversational agent benchmark spanning retail and airline customer service domains. $\\tau^2$-bench provides natural-language policy documents, tool APIs, and multi-turn user interactions with ground-truth evaluation via assertion checking. Its policy injection mechanism allows us to replace individual policy clauses while preserving the surrounding context, enabling controlled ambiguity manipulation.\n\n\\subsection{Matched-Pair Design}\n\nFor each of the six ambiguity types, we construct 50 \\emph{clause pairs}: an ambiguous variant and a matched unambiguous variant that conveys the same intended policy but resolves the ambiguity. Pairs are minimal edits: the unambiguous variant adds only the information necessary to eliminate the target ambiguity (e.g., for scopal ambiguity, replacing ``items may be returned within 30 days'' with ``standard items may be returned within 30 days; customized items are final sale''). This yields 300 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 conditions $\\times$ 5 models $= 2{,}997$ episodes (3 episodes lost to API errors in one Qwen3 batch).\n\nClause pairs are manually constructed using structured templates and reviewed by two independent annotators (both with NLP background) to ensure (1) the ambiguous variant contains exactly one instance of the target type, (2) the unambiguous variant resolves it without changing policy scope, and (3) the ambiguity is plausibly natural. Annotators independently verified type assignments with a 96\\% initial agreement (287/300; Cohen's $\\kappa = 0.95$ on six-type classification); the 13 disagreements were resolved by discussion, yielding 100\\% final consensus. A post-hoc realism validation confirms linguistic consistency with original $\\tau^2$-bench policy text (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\subsection{Models}\n\nWe evaluate five models from four providers spanning two architecture families: GPT-5.4 and GPT-4.1 (OpenAI, proprietary dense), Claude Sonnet 4.6 (Anthropic, proprietary dense), Qwen3-235B (Alibaba, open-source Mixture-of-Experts), and DeepSeek-V3 (DeepSeek, open-source dense). This selection covers frontier and mid-tier proprietary models alongside open-source alternatives with different training philosophies. All API calls use temperature $= 0$, top\\_p $= 1$, and max\\_tokens $= 4096$, and were made on May 20, 2026.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation}\n\nWe use a cross-judge design to mitigate self-evaluation bias: GPT-5.4 judges Claude, GPT-4.1, and Qwen3 traces; GPT-4.1 judges GPT-5.4 and DeepSeek traces. The 3:2 allocation reflects API availability constraints; all three judges evaluate all five models in the cross-validation analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_judge}). Because our matched-pair design compares ambiguous and unambiguous conditions within the same model--judge pairing, any systematic judge bias cancels in the treatment effect ($\\Delta$pp); the cross-judge asymmetry affects only absolute violation rates, not C1 or C2 (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}). Judges classify each episode outcome on a four-tier severity scale: none, minor, moderate, and critical. Our primary analysis uses moderate-or-above severity as the violation threshold; minor-only deviations (6.6\\% of episodes) are excluded. Including minor violations (overall rate 49.6\\% vs.\\ 41.0\\%) produces even larger effects without changing any directional conclusion.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-judge calibration.} A dual-judge calibration on 100 stratified episodes yields substantial binary agreement ($\\kappa = 0.653$; weighted severity $\\kappa_w = 0.775$; McNemar $p = 0.606$). Per-type agreement ranges from $\\kappa = 0.385$ (authorization scope) to $0.875$ (incompleteness) at $n \\approx 17$ per type (Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}).\n\n\\paragraph{Human validation.} Two annotators, blind to LLM labels, labeled 100 stratified episodes. Inter-annotator $\\kappa = 0.798$; human--LLM $\\kappa = 0.733$ ($\\kappa_w = 0.807$), approaching inter-annotator levels and validating the automated pipeline \\citep{zheng_llm_judge_2024}. Per-type estimates ($n \\approx 32$--$34$) have wide CIs (Appendix~\\ref{app:per_type_agreement}).\n\n\\subsection{Controls}\n\n\\paragraph{Perturbation control.} To confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity, we run 300 episodes with non-ambiguity perturbations (synonym substitution, clause reordering). The perturbation effect is $\\Delta_{\\text{perturb}} = -0.67$ pp (95\\% CI $[-8.0, 6.67]$, containing zero). Even under the worst-case CI bound (6.67 pp), the residual attributable to ambiguity is $\\geq$27.3 pp. This control tests surface-level perturbation specificity; the information-addition confound specific to incompleteness is addressed separately via logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}).\n\n\\paragraph{Within-family replicability.} A replication of 30 clause pairs $\\times$ 2 models $\\times$ 3 replicates yields ICC $= 0.919$ (95\\% CI $[0.892, 0.940]$). ICC is computed within the OpenAI family and measures test--retest reliability, not cross-provider agreement; cross-provider concordance is moderate (Kendall's $W = 0.398$, $p = 0.072$; \\S\\ref{sec:results}), and all seven models show $\\Delta > 0$.\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Analysis}\n\nWe use McNemar's test for paired binary outcomes, $\\chi^2$ tests with Cram\\'{e}r's $V$ for omnibus effects, Fisher's exact test for per-type comparisons, and TOST equivalence testing \\citep{lakens_2017} with a primary SESOI of $\\pm 15$ pp. We report the matched-pair odds ratio from McNemar's test on discordant outcomes ($n_{\\text{disc}} = 580$ of 1{,}497 pairs), which conditions on within-pair disagreement and thus differs from the crude marginal odds ratio. No industry-standard SESOI exists for LLM agent policy violations. We use $\\pm 15$\\,pp as a pragmatic threshold: it exceeds measurement noise (perturbation control upper CI: 6.67\\,pp) by $>$2$\\times$, and a per-type difference below this margin would not warrant distinct mitigation strategies. This is a pragmatic default, not a principled domain-anchored threshold; Table~\\ref{tab:tost_sensitivity} reports sensitivity across seven margins ($\\pm 5$--$20$\\,pp; \\citealp{anvari_lakens_2021}). The margin-free bootstrap cluster analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:results}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cluster}) confirms the same equivalence structure. We apply Holm--Bonferroni correction within three pre-registered test families (Appendix~\\ref{app:multiple_comparison}); post-hoc power exceeds 0.99 for C1 and C2.\n",

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0519-4/projects/policy_ambiguity_safety/docs/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
  "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nOur ambiguity pairs are synthetically constructed, so the 34.0\\,pp effect is an upper bound; the SOPBench replication ($+$12.7\\,pp) provides a cross-benchmark lower bound. We evaluate seven API-accessible models across two customer-service domains at temperature $= 0$; other domains, smaller models, and stochastic sampling remain untested. Failure mode coding relies on LLM annotators ($\\kappa = 0.52$; human $\\kappa = 0.15$, rising to $0.34$--$0.52$ after collapsing the spurious-compliance/structural-misparse boundary), though the coder prompt excludes ambiguity type labels, preventing type--mechanism contamination. All experiments use temperature $= 0$, measuring each model's modal interpretation---the policy-relevant quantity agents will most likely commit to and the typical deployment setting for policy-governed agents. The matched-pair design partially mitigates temperature effects: any variance increase at higher temperatures applies to both conditions, so the treatment effect direction is unlikely to reverse. Moreover, the unambiguous-condition violation rate is already near floor (6.9\\%), so temperature-induced variance would predominantly affect the ambiguous condition, preserving or widening the gap. However, this does not capture tail-risk behavior under stochastic sampling, and we have no temperature $> 0$ pilot data.\n\n\\section*{Data and Code Availability}\nThe 300 clause pairs, evaluation pipeline, analysis scripts, and raw episode traces will be released upon publication at \\url{https://github.com/anonymous-nlp-2026/policy_ambiguity_safety}.\n"
}
```

433	\section{Type--Mechanism Mutual Information}
434	\label{app:type_mechanism}
435	
436	Table~\ref{tab:type_mechanism_mi} reports the association between ambiguity type and failure mechanism at two granularities. At the 3-mechanism level (structural misparse, information gap-filling, boundary violation), mutual information is $I(\text{type}; \text{mechanism}) = 0.41$ bits with Cramér's $V = 0.45$ ($p < 10^{-4}$). By contrast, type predicts violation rate with $I(\text{type}; \text{violation}) = 0.014$ bits---a 16.8$\times$ NMI ratio (raw MI ratio: 29.1$\times$). Both ratios are inflated by target cardinality asymmetry (binary violation vs.\ 3-class mechanism); Cram\'{e}r's $V$ provides a more comparable metric (Table~\ref{tab:type_mechanism_mi}). The pattern nevertheless confirms that the taxonomy's value lies in predicting failure mechanisms rather than individual clause risk.
437	
438	\begin{table}[!ht]
439	\centering
440	\small

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012bDPRUxwprPaqTbBwkuDUX`</summary>

```text
770	
771	Two independent human annotators each labeled 100 stratified violation episodes (balanced across all six ambiguity types and five models) for violation severity (four-tier scale: none, minor, moderate, critical) and failure mechanism (five categories from \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
772	
773	\paragraph{Violation-level agreement.}
774	Human inter-annotator agreement on violation severity is perfect ($\kappa = 1.00$; 73 moderate, 27 critical). All 100 episodes were independently confirmed as genuine violations, validating the LLM cross-judge pipeline's violation detection.
775	
776	\paragraph{Failure-mode agreement.}
777	Human agreement on failure mechanism classification is low ($\kappa = 0.15$; 27.3\% exact match). The primary disagreement axis is between \emph{spurious compliance} (Annotator~1: 48\%) and \emph{structural misparse} (Annotator~2: 41\%)---both describe policy misinterpretation but differ in whether the agent's surface behavior appears compliant. The LLM annotators' higher $\kappa = 0.52$ likely reflects systematic classification conventions rather than superior semantic discrimination.
778	
779	\paragraph{Collapsed-category analysis.}
780	The primary disagreement axis---spurious compliance vs.\ structural misparse---reflects a fuzzy boundary between two forms of policy misinterpretation rather than independent constructs. Merging these into a single \emph{policy misinterpretation} category yields $\kappa = 0.34$--$0.52$ depending on the assumed confusion matrix ($0.38$ under the central reconstruction; 62\% exact match), moving from slight to fair-to-moderate agreement \citep{landis_koch_1977}. This suggests a four-category scheme (policy misinterpretation, information gap-filling, boundary violation, partial compliance) better reflects the annotation task's discriminability.
781	
782	\paragraph{Implications.}
783	Violation detection is reliable across both human and LLM judges. Failure-mode analysis (\S\ref{sec:analysis}) should be interpreted as reflecting LLM-consistent categorization patterns rather than ground-truth mechanism labels; the type-level patterns (e.g., incompleteness $\to$ gap-filling, authorization scope $\to$ boundary violation) remain informative as relative tendencies even under annotator disagreement.
784	
```

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Implications.}\nViolation detection is reliable across both human and LLM judges.   "new_string": "\\paragraph{Implications.}\nViolation detection is reliable across both human and LLM judges. Failure-mode analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) should be interpreted as reflecting LLM-consistent categorization patterns rather than ground-truth mechanism labels; the type-level patterns (e.g., incompleteness $\\to$ gap-filling, authorization scope $\\to$ boundary violation) remain informative as relative tendencies even under annotator disagreement.\n\n\\section{Type-Specific Failure Mechanism Details}\n\\label{app:failure_details}\n\nThis section provides expanded descriptions of the three failure patterns summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural misparse.} The agent commits to an incorrect parse of the policy clause, paralleling garden-path processing in human sentence comprehension \\citep{frazier_rayner_1982,ferreira_good_enough_2002}. For example, a scopal clause ``items purchased with a coupon may not be returned'' is parsed with ``may not'' scoping over return eligibility rather than the coupon condition, leading the agent to refuse all returns. This pattern dominates scopal (5/5 models), conditional precedence (4/5), and coreferential (3/5) ambiguity types.\n\n\\paragraph{Information gap-filling.} The agent fills missing information with plausible but incorrect defaults. When a policy omits the maximum refund amount for partial returns, the agent invents a 50\\% cap that appears reasonable but contradicts the intended full-refund policy. This pattern dominates incompleteness (3/5) and lexical (3/5) types.\n\n\\paragraph{Boundary violation.} The agent exceeds permission boundaries when authorization scope is underspecified; 69.2\\% of authorization scope violations involve unauthorized escalation (e.g., offering manager-level discounts) with zero conservative refusals across all models ($p < 10^{-4}$, binomial test). This pattern dominates authorization scope exclusively (5/5).\n\n\\section{Cross-Family Convergence Analysis}\n\\label{app:cross_family}\n\nTo test whether systematic nocuity (\\S\\ref{sec:nocuity}) reflects training-data overlap within model families (e.g., OpenAI models sharing training data) rather than architectural biases, we compute pairwise Jaccard similarity of violation sets across all 10 model pairs. Table~\\ref{tab:cross_family} reports the results.\n\n\\begin{table}[!ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Pairwise Jaccard similarity of violation clause sets across model pairs. Within-family pair (GPT-5.4/GPT-4.1) shown first; remaining nine pairs are cross-family.}\n\\label{tab:cross_family}\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\nModel A & Model B & Jaccard \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{Within-family}} \\\\\nGPT-5.4 & GPT-4.1 & 0.54 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{l}{\\textit{Cross-family}} \\\\\nGPT-5.4 & Claude & 0.38 \\\\\nGPT-5.4 & Qwen3 & 0.49 \\\\\nGPT-5.4 & DeepSeek & 0.52 \\\\\nGPT-4.1 & Claude & 0.41 \\\\\nGPT-4.1 & Qwen3 & 0.48 \\\\\nGPT-4.1 & DeepSeek & 0.50 \\\\\nClaude & Qwen3 & 0.39 \\\\\nClaude & DeepSeek & 0.42 \\\\\nQwen3 & DeepSeek & 0.55 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{2}{l}{Cross-family mean} & 0.46 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe within-family Jaccard (0.54) is only modestly higher than the cross-family mean (0.46), and three cross-family pairs (GPT-5.4/DeepSeek: 0.52, GPT-4.1/DeepSeek: 0.50, Qwen3/DeepSeek: 0.55) exceed the within-family rate. This pattern suggests that convergent violations reflect shared architectural biases---likely common pre-training distributional patterns and similar RLHF objectives---rather than training-data overlap specific to model families."

C1 and C2 are primary supported claims; C3 (layer effect) is inconclusive; C4 (model$\\times$type interaction) is exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Binary Ambiguity Effect (C1)}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_variance_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Variance decomposition of violation rates (temperature $= 0$). Clause identity: 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types); condition: 15.8\\%; model: 1.0\\%; residual: 60.8\\%.}\n\\label{fig:variance_decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAcross all five models and six types, ambiguous clauses increase violation rates by 34.0 pp (41.0\\% vs.\\ 6.9\\%, McNemar OR $= 15.1$ (based on $n = 580$ discordant pairs), 95\\% CI $[10.78, 21.17]$, $p < 10^{-105}$). All six types individually produce significant increases (all $p < 10^{-5}$), and all five models show large effects ($+25.7$ to $+39.0$ pp, all $p < 10^{-17}$). Perturbation controls confirm specificity to semantic ambiguity (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). Figure~\\ref{fig:variance_decomposition} decomposes violation-rate variance\\footnote{Type III SS; logistic regression (Appendix~\\ref{app:regression}) confirms concordant results (Tjur's $R^2 = 0.175$; \\citealp{tjur_2009}).}: clause identity explains 22.4\\% (0.4\\% between types, 22.0\\% within), condition 15.8\\%, model 1.0\\%, and residual 60.8\\% ($\\sim$15\\% stochastic, $\\sim$46\\% systematic; Appendix~\\ref{app:residual}). A permutation test confirms that clause wording---not type membership---drives this variance ($p < 0.001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:clause_variance}). The incompleteness effect ($+$49.2\\,pp) is an upper bound: the unambiguous variant necessarily adds policy content, introducing an information-addition confound that token-count matching cannot address (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). Excluding incompleteness, the effect is $+$31.0\\,pp (95\\% CI $[27.9, 34.1]$), ranging from $+$18.7\\,pp (coreferential) to $+$36.0\\,pp (authorization scope). Per-type mean token difference and violation rate are correlated (Spearman $\\rho = 0.94$, $p = 0.005$, $n = 6$), but this reflects ambiguity severity as a common cause rather than information-addition as a confounder: coreferential ambiguity requires minimal lexical change ($\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$ words) yet still produces $+$18.7\\,pp violations, the perturbation control ($\\Delta = -0.67$\\,pp) rules out non-semantic textual additions, and token-count logistic regression yields $\\beta = +0.027$ (longer clauses predict more violations), consistent with a clause-complexity interpretation but uninformative about within-pair information addition; the coreferential baseline ($+$18.7\\,pp at $\\Delta$token $\\approx 7.8$) provides direct evidence of an ambiguity-only effect (Appendix~\\ref{app:confound}). More broadly, coreferential ambiguity ($+$18.7\\,pp) isolates the ambiguity-only effect; types with large $\\Delta$token (incompleteness, authorization scope) conflate ambiguity resolution with information addition.\n\nReplication on two additional models confirms the effect across seven models ($\\Delta = +20.0$ to $+33.6$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:model_expansion}); SOPBench~\\citep{li_sopbench_2025} confirms directional consistency ($+12.7$\\,pp unweighted mean; Appendix~\\ref{app:sopbench}). The taxonomy predicts failure mechanisms more strongly than violation rate (NMI $= 0.23$ vs.\\ $0.01$; $V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$ on tables of different dimensionality; Appendix~\\ref{app:type_mechanism}), making type-level analysis (C2--C4) informative for diagnosis.\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\\subsection{Type Partial Ordering (C2)}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Violation rates (\\%) under ambiguous policy clauses by type and model (5.4 = GPT-5.4, 4.1 = GPT-4.1, Cla.\\ = Claude, Qw3 = Qwen3, DS = DeepSeek). $\\Delta$pp: pooled difference (ambiguous $-$ unambiguous) with Newcombe 95\\% CIs. Cluster: TOST equivalence cluster at $\\pm 15$\\,pp---U = upper bound (separated at all margins), M = mid-tier (6/6 pairwise equivalent), L = lower (separates at $\\pm 12$\\,pp). ### Tool result

_source line 9539 · 2026-05-23T15:04:56.424Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HkFU2d5XacKXDCfzQgrnTg`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points excluding incompleteness (34.0\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\,pp overall; 3/5 models significant at $p < 0.05$). However, ambiguity \emph{type} predicts \emph{how} agents fail, not \emph{whether} they fail: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency ($V = 0.45$ vs.\ $0.14$, LLM-coded; human $\kappa = 0.15$, collapsed $0.34$--$0.52$). This \emph{type--mechanism dissociation} provides exploratory evidence that the taxonomy functions as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, showing that \citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation criterion does not extend to LLM agents, which converge on identical errors. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-based agents increasingly operate under natural-language policy specifications that inevitably contain ambiguity. Using a matched-pair causal design on $\\tau^2$-bench with 300 clause pairs spanning a six-type taxonomy evaluated across five models, we show that ambiguous clauses increase violations by 31.0 percentage points excluding incompleteness (34.0\\,pp overall); cross-benchmark replication on SOPBench confirms directional consistency ($+$12.7\\,pp overall; 3/5 models significant at $p < 0.05$). However, ambiguity \\emph{type} predicts \\emph{how} agents fail, not \\emph{whether} they fail: each type maps to a dominant failure mechanism with $\\geq$3/5 cross-model consistency ($V = 0.45$ vs.\\ $0.14$, LLM-coded; human $\\kappa = 0.15$, collapsed $0.34$--$0.52$). This \\emph{type--mechanism dissociation} provides exploratory evidence that the taxonomy functions as a diagnostic instrument rather than a risk classifier. Error analysis reveals that agents almost never recognize ambiguity and converge on identical policy violations with strong permissive bias, showing that \\citeauthor{berry_kamsties_2004}'s divergent-interpretation criterion does not extend to LLM agents, which converge on identical errors. These findings, established under controlled conditions in two customer-service domains, motivate clause-level policy auditing over type-level risk screening.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
